62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:58.333333333333336%;--col-max-width:58.333333333333336%;}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction</strong></p><p>We recently discovered an <code>APT</code> campaign we are calling <code>Operation Dragon Castling</code>. The campaign is targeting what appears to be betting companies in <code>South East Asia</code>, more specifically companies located in <code>Taiwan</code>, the <code>Philippines</code>, and <code>Hong Kong</code>. With moderate confidence, we can attribute the campaign to a <code>Chinese speaking APT group</code>, but unfortunately cannot attribute the attack to a specific group and are not sure what the attackers are after.</p><p>We found notable code similarity between one of the modules used by this APT group (the <code>MulCom backdoor</code>) and the <code>FFRat</code> samples described by the <code>BlackBerry Cylance Threat Research Team</code> in their <code>2017</code> <a href="https://blogs.blackberry.com/en/2017/06/breaking-down-ff-rat-malware">report</a> and <code>Palo Alto Networks</code> in their <code>2015</code> <a href="https://researchcenter.paloaltonetworks.com/2015/04/unit-42-identifies-new-dragonok-backdoor-malware-deployed-against-japanese-targets/">report</a>. Based on this, we suspect that the FFRat codebase is being shared between several Chinese adversary groups. Unfortunately, this is not sufficient for attribution as FFRat itself was never reliably attributed.</p><p>In this blogpost we will describe the malware used in these attacks and the backdoor planted by the APT group, as well as other malicious files used to gain persistence and access to the infected machines. We will also discuss the two infection vectors we saw being used to deliver the malware: an infected installer and exploitation of a vulnerable legitimate application, <code>WPS</code> <code>Office</code>.</p><p>We identified a new vulnerability (<a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934">CVE-2022-24934</a>) in the WPS Office updater wpsupdate.exe, which we suspect that the attackers abused.</p><p>We would like to thank Taiwan’s <a href="https://teamt5.org/en/"><code>TeamT5</code></a> for providing us with IoCs related to the infection vector.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=xtgtPzOl 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=yFwwuMuO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=R6dvwU2H 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=PbYqrQoO 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=z8ERjtcT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=Z2JP1HN8 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Ffinal.drawio.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dz8ERjtcT&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Ffinal.drawio.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dz8ERjtcT&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Ffinal.drawio.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dz8ERjtcT&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>In the diagram above, we describe the relations between the malicious files. Some of the relations might not be accurate, e.g. we are not entirely sure if the MulCom backdoor is loaded by the <code>CorePlugin</code>. However, we strongly believe that it is one of the malicious files used in this campaign.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Infection Vector</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen multiple infection vectors used in this campaign. Among others, an attacker sent an email with an infected installer to the support team of one of the targeted companies asking to check for a bug in their software. In this post, we are going to describe another vector we’ve seen: a fake <code>WPS Office</code> update package. We suspect an attacker exploited a bug in the WPS updater<code> wpsupdate.exe</code>, which is a part of the WPS Office installation package. We have contacted WPS Office team about the vulnerability (<a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934"><code>CVE-2022-24934</code></a>), which we discovered, and it has since been fixed.</p><p>During our investigation we saw suspicious behavior in the WPS updater process. When analyzing the binary we discovered a potential security issue that allows an attacker to use the updater to communicate with a server controlled by the attacker to perform actions on the victim’s system, including downloading and running arbitrary executables. To exploit the vulnerability, a registry key under <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER </code>needs to be modified, and by doing this an attacker gains persistence on the system and control over the update process. In the case we analyzed, the malicious binary was downloaded from the domain <code>update.wps[.]cn</code>, which is a domain belonging to <code>Kingsoft</code>, but the serving IP (<code>103.140.187.16</code>) has no relationship to the company, so we assume that it is a fake update server used by the attackers.&nbsp;<br>The downloaded binary (<code>setup_CN_2052_11.1.0.8830_PersonalDownload_Triale.exe - B9BEA7D1822D9996E0F04CB5BF5103C48828C5121B82E3EB9860E7C4577E2954</code>) drops two files for sideloading: a signed <code>QMSpeedupRocketTrayInjectHelper64.exe - Tencent Technology (a3f3bc958107258b3aa6e9e959377dfa607534cc6a426ee8ae193b463483c341)</code> and a malicious DLL<code> QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll.</code></p><h2><strong>Dropper 1 (QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll)</strong></h2><p>76adf4fd93b70c4dece4b536b4fae76793d9aa7d8d6ee1750c1ad1f0ffa75491</p><p>The first stage is a backdoor communicating with a C&amp;C (<code>mirrors.centos.8788912[.]com</code>). Before contacting the C&amp;C server, the backdoor performs several preparational operations. It hooks three functions: <code>GetProcAddress</code>, <code>FreeLibrary</code>, <code>LdrUnloadDll</code>.&nbsp;To get the C&amp;C domain, it maps itself to the memory and reads data starting at the offset <code>1064 </code>from the end. The domain name is not encrypted in any way and is stored as a wide string in clear text in the binary.&nbsp;</p><p>Then it initializes an object for a <code>JScript</code> class with the named item <code>ScriptHelper</code>.&nbsp; The dropper uses the <code>ImpersonateLoggedOnUser </code>API Call to re-use a token from <code>explorer.exe</code> so it effectively runs under the same user. Additionally, it uses <code>RegOverridePredefKey </code>to redirect the current <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER </code>to <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER&nbsp; </code>of an impersonated user.&nbsp;For communication with C&amp;C it constructs a UserAgent string with some system information e.g. <code>Mozilla/4.0 (compatible; MSIE 9.0; Windows NT 6.1;.NET CLR 2.0). The information that is exfiltrated is: Internet Explorer version, Windows version, the value of the “<em>User Agent\Post Platform”</em></code><em> </em>registry values.</p><p>After that, the sample constructs <code>JScript </code>code to execute. The header of the code contains definitions of two variables: <code>server</code> with the C&amp;C domain name and a hardcoded <code>key</code>. Then it sends the HTTP <code>GET </code>request to <code>/api/connect,</code> the response should be encrypted <code>JScript </code>code that is decrypted, appended to the constructed header and executed using the <code>JScript </code>class created previously.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At the time of analysis, the C&amp;C was not responding, but from the telemetry data we can conclude that it was downloading the next stage from <code>hxxp://mirrors.centos.8788912.com/upload/ea76ad28a3916f52a748a4f475700987.exe</code> to <code>%ProgramData%\icbc_logtmp.exe</code> and executing it.</p><h2><strong>Dropper 2 (IcbcLog)</strong></h2><p>a428351dcb235b16dc5190c108e6734b09c3b7be93c0ef3d838cf91641b328b3</p><p>The second dropper is a runner that, when executed, tries to escalate privileges via the <code>COM Session Moniker Privilege </code>Escalation <code>(</code><a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/security-updates/securitybulletins/2017/ms17-012"><code>MS17-012</code></a><code>)</code>, then dropping a few binaries, which are stored with the following resource IDs:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-27%20at%209.11.05%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=4J9bzYuO 1x, 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.11.05%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DbqxfmsKU&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The encrypted payloads have the following structure:</p><p>The encryption key is a wide string starting from offset <code>0x8</code>. The encrypted data starts at the offset <code>0x528</code>. To decrypt the data, a SHA256 hash of the key is created using <code>CryptHashData </code>API, and is then used with a hard-coded IV<code> 0123456789abcde</code> to decrypt the data using <code>CryptDecrypt </code>API with the <code>AES256</code> algorithm. After that, the decrypted data is decompressed with <code>RtlDecompressBuffer</code>. To verify that the decryption went well, the <code>CRC32</code> of the data is computed and compared to the value at the offset <code>0x4 </code>of the original resource data. When all the payloads are dropped to the disk, <code>bdservicehost.exe </code>is executed to run the next stage.</p><h2><strong>Loader (CoreX)</strong></h2><p>97c392ca71d11de76b69d8bf6caf06fa3802d0157257764a0e3d6f0159436c42</p><p>The <code>Loader (CoreX) </code>DLL is sideloaded during the previous stage <code>(Dropper 2)</code> and acts as a dropper. Similarly to <code>Dropper 1</code>, it hooks the <code>GetProcAddress </code>and <code>FreeLibrary </code>API functions. These hooks execute the main code of this library. The main code first checks whether it was loaded by <code>regsvr32.exe</code> and then it retrieves encrypted data from its resources. This data is dropped into the same folder as <code>syscfg.dat</code>. The file is then loaded and decrypted using AES-256 with the following options for setup:</p><ul><li>Key is the computer name and IV is <code>qwertyui12345678</code></li><li>AES-256 setup parameters are embedded in the resource in the format <code>&lt;key&gt;#&lt;IV&gt;</code>. So you may e.g. see <code>cbfc2vyuzckloknf#8o3yfn0uee429m8d</code></li></ul></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=7IaymrJj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=bunxx5md 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=nkdVNpiP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=LdUH3tWJ 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">AES-256 setup parameters</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The main code continues to check if the process <code>ekrn.exe</code> is running. <code>ekrn.exe</code> is an ESET Kernel service. If the ESET Kernel service is running, it will try to remap <code>ntdll.dll</code>. We assume that this is used to bypass <code>ntdll.dll</code> hooking.&nbsp;</p><p>After a service check, it will decompress and execute shellcode, which in turn loads a DLL with the next stage. The DLL is stored, unencrypted, as part of the shellcode.&nbsp;The shellcode enumerates exports of <code>ntdll.dll </code>and builds an array with hashes of names of all <code>Zw*</code> functions (windows native API system calls) then sorts them by their RVA. By doing this, the shellcode exploits the fact that the order of RVAs of <code>Zw*</code> functions equals the order of the corresponding syscalls, so an index of the <code>Zw*</code> function in this array is a syscall number, which can be called using the syscall instruction. Security solutions can therefore be bypassed based on the hooking of the API in userspace. Finally, the embedded core module DLL is loaded and executed.</p><h2><strong>Proto8 (Core module)</strong></h2><p>f3ed09ee3fe869e76f34eee1ef974d1b24297a13a58ebff20ea4541b9a2d86c7</p><p>The core module is a single DLL that is responsible for setting up the malware’s working directory, loading configuration files, updating its code, loading plugins, beaconing to C&amp;C servers and waiting for commands.</p><p>It has a cascading structure with four steps:</p><h3><strong>Step 1</strong></h3><p>The first part is dedicated to initial checks and a few evasion techniques.&nbsp;At first, the core module verifies that the DLL is being run by <code>spdlogd.exe</code> (an executable used for persistence, see below) or that it is not being run by <code>rundll32.exe.</code> If this check fails, the execution terminates. The DLL proceeds by hooking the <code>GetProcAddress </code>and <code>FreeLibrary </code>functions in order to execute the main function, similarly to the previous infection stages.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The malware then creates a new window (named <code>Sample</code>) with a custom callback function. A message with the ID <code>0x411 </code>is sent to the window via <code>SendMessageW</code> which causes the aforementioned callback to execute the main function. The callback function can also process the <code>0x412 </code>message ID, even though no specific functionality is tied to it.</p></div><div for 0x412 as well</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>Step 2</strong></h3><p>In the second step, the module tries to self-update, load configuration files and set up its working directory (WD).</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h4><strong>Self-update</strong></h4><p>The malware first looks for a file called <code>new_version.dat</code> – if it exists, its content is loaded into memory, executed in a new thread and a debug string <code>“run code ok”</code> is printed out. We did not come across this file, but based on its name and context, this is most likely a self update functionality.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=htarqsb- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=tNyX60xH 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=rzOWTO5X 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=k4c-0zTd 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=1mGImiZm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=zgl4xZYW 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Load configuration file <code>inst.dat </code>and set up working directory. First, the core module configuration file inst.dat is searched for in the following three locations:</p><ul><li>the directory where the core module DLL is located</li><li>the directory where the EXE that loaded the core module DLL it is located</li><li><code>C:\ProgramData\</code></li></ul><p>It contains the path to the malware’s working directory in plaintext. If it is not found, a hard-coded directory name is used and the directory is created. The working directory is a location the malware uses to drop or read any files it uses in subsequent execution phases.</p><p>Load configuration file <code>smcache.dat</code>.</p><p>After the working directory is set up, the sample will load the configuration file <code>smcache.dat</code> from it. This file contains the domains, protocols and port numbers used to communicate with C&amp;C servers (details in Step 4) plus a <code>“comment”</code> string. This string is likely used to identify the campaign or individual victims. It is used to create an empty file on the victim’s computer (see below) and it’s also sent as a part of the initial beacon when communicating with C&amp;C servers. We refer to it as the<code> “comment string”</code> because we have seen a few versions of smcache.dat where the content of the string was<code> “the comment string here”</code> and it is also present in another configuration file with the name <code>comment.dat</code> which has the INI file format and contains this string under the key COMMENT.</p><p>Create a <code>log </code>file</p><p>Right after the sample finds and reads smcache.dat, it creates a file based on the victim’s username and the comment string from smcache.dat. If the comment string is not present, it will use a default hard-coded value (for example <code>M86_99.lck</code>). Based on the extension it could be a log of some sort, but we haven’t seen any part of the malware writing into it so it could just serve as a lockfile. After the file is successfully created, the malware creates a mutex and goes on to the next step.</p><h3><strong>Step 3</strong></h3><p>Next, the malware collects information about the infected environment (such as username, DNS and NetBios computer names as well as OS version and architecture) and sets up its internal structures, most notably a list of <code>“call objects”</code>. Call objects are structures each associated with a particular function and saved into a <code>“dispatcher”</code> structure in a map with hard-coded 4-byte keys. These keys are later used to call the functions based on commands from C&amp;C servers.&nbsp;</p><p>The key values (IDs) seem to be structured, where the first three bytes are always the same within a given sample, while the last byte is always the same for a given usage across all the core module samples that we’ve seen. For example, the function that calls the <code>RevertToSelf </code>function is identified by the number <code>0x20210326 </code>in some versions of the core module that we’ve seen and<code> <em>0x19181726</em> </code>in others. This suggests that the first three bytes of the ID number are tied to the core module version, or more likely the infrastructure version, while the last byte is the actual ID of a function.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Webdav&nbsp;</strong></p><p>While initializing the call objects the core module also tries to connect to the URL <code>hxxps://dav.jianguoyun.com/dav/</code> with the username <code>12121jhksdf </code>and password <code>121121212 </code>by calling <code>WNetAddConnection3W</code>. This address was not responsive at the time of analysis but <code>jianguoyun[.]com</code> is a Chinese file sharing service. Our hypothesis is that this is either a way to get plugin code or an updated version of the core module itself.</p><p><strong>Plugins</strong></p><p>The core module contains a function that receives a buffer with plugin DLL data, saves it into a file with the name <code>kbg&lt;tick_count&gt;.dat</code> in the malware working directory, loads it into memory and then calls its exported function <code>InitCorePlug</code>. The plugin file on disk is set to be deleted on reboot by calling <code>MoveFileExW </code>with the parameter <code>MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT</code>.&nbsp;For more information about the plugins, see the dedicated Plugins section.</p><h3><strong>Step 4</strong></h3><p>In the final step, the malware will iterate over C&amp;C servers contained in the smcache.dat configuration file and will try to reach each one.&nbsp;The structure of the <code>smcache.dat</code> config file is as follows:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fstruct.png.webp%3Fitok%3DT7a75OwH&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">The structure of the smcache.dat config file</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The protocol string can have one of nine possible values:&nbsp;</p><ul><li><code>TCP</code></li><li><code>HTTPS</code></li><li><code>UDP</code></li><li><code>DNS</code></li><li><code>ICMP</code></li><li><code>HTTPSIPV6</code></li><li><code>WEB</code></li><li><code>SSH</code></li><li><code>HTTP</code></li></ul><p>Depending on the protocol tied to the particular C&amp;C domain, the malware sets up the connection, sends a beacon to the C&amp;C and waits for commands.</p><p>In this blogpost, we will mainly focus on the HTTP protocol option as we’ve seen it being used by the attackers.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FHTTP-vftable.png.webp%3Fitok%3DZ-Gw1M9X&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>When using the HTTP protocol, the core module first opens two persistent request handles – one for <code>POST </code>and one for <code>GET </code>requests, both to <code>“/connect”</code>. These handles are tested by sending an empty buffer in the <code>POST </code>request and checking the HTTP status code of the <code>GET </code>request. Following this, the malware sends the initial beacon to the C&amp;C server by calling the <code>InternetWriteFile </code>API with the previously opened <code>POST </code>request handle and reads data from the <code>GET </code>request handle by calling <code>InternetReadFile</code>.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/wireshark-1.png.webp?itok=onT7ziV5 1x, data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/wireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp?itok=xl9cXemc 1x, beacon" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">HTTP POST beacon</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The core module uses the following (mostly hard-coded) HTTP headers:</p><ul><li><code>Accept: */*</code></li><li><code>x-cid: {<em>&lt;uuid&gt;</em>}</code> – new uuid is generated for each <code>GET/POST </code>request pair</li><li><code>Pragma: no-cache</code></li><li><code>Cache-control: no-transform</code></li><li><code>User-Agent: &lt;user_agent&gt;</code> – generated from registry or hard-coded (see below)</li><li><code>Host: &lt;host_value&gt;</code> – C&amp;C server domain or the value from hostcfg.dat (see below)</li><li><code>Connection: Keep-Alive</code></li><li><code>Content-Length: 4294967295</code> (max uint, only in the POST request)</li></ul><p><strong>User-Agent header</strong></p><p>The User-Agent string is constructed from the registry the same way as in the <code>Dropper 1</code> module (including the logged-on user impersonation when accessing registry) or a hard-coded string is used if the registry access fails: <code>“Mozilla/4.0 (compatible; MSIE 8.0; Windows NT 6.1; WOW64; Trident/4.0; SLCC2; .NET CLR 2.0.50727; .NET CLR 3.5.30729; .NET CLR 3.0.30729; Media Center PC 6.0)”</code>.</p><p><strong>Host header</strong></p><p>When setting up this header, the malware looks for either a resource with the<code> ID 1816</code> or a file called <code>hostcfg.dat</code> if the resource is not found. If the resource or file is found, the content is used as the value in the <code>Host </code>HTTP header for all C&amp;C communication instead of the C&amp;C domain found in <code>smcache.dat</code>. It does not change the actual C&amp;C domain to which the request is made – this suggests the possibility of the C&amp;C server being behind a reverse proxy.</p><p><strong>Initial beacon</strong></p><p>The first data packet the malware sends to a C&amp;C server contains a base64 encoded LZNT1-compressed buffer, including a newly generated uuid (different from the uuid used in the x-cid header), the victim’s username, OS version and architecture, computer DNS and BIOS names and the comment string found in <code>smcache.dat </code>or<code> comment.dat</code>. The value from <code>comment.dat</code> takes precedence if this file exists.<em>&nbsp;</em></p><p>In the core module sample we analyzed, there was actually a typo in the function that reads the value from<code> comment.dat </code>– it looks for the key<code> “COMMNET”</code> instead of <code>“COMMENT”</code>.</p><p>After this, the malware enters a loop waiting for commands from the C&amp;C server in the form of the ID value of one of the call objects.<br>Each message sent to the C&amp;C server contains a hard-coded four byte number value with the same structure as the values used as keys in the call-object map. The ID numbers associated with messages sent to C&amp;C servers that we’ve seen are:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Interesting observations about the protocols, other than the HTTP protocol:</p><ul><li>HTTPS does not use persistent request handles</li><li>HTTPS uses HTTP <code>GET </code>request with data Base64-encoded in the cookie header to send the initial beacon</li><li>HTTPS, TCP and UDP use a custom “magic” header: <code>Magic-Code: hhjjdfgh</code></li></ul><h3><strong>General observations on the core module</strong></h3></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The core samples we observed often output debug strings via <code>OutputDebugStringA </code>and <code>OutputDebugStringW </code>or by sending them to the C&amp;C server. Examples of debug strings used by the core module are: its filepath at the beginning of execution, <code>“run code ok”</code> after self-update, <code>“In googo”</code> in the hook of <code>GetProcAddress</code>, <code>“recv bomb”</code> and <code>“sent bomb”</code> in the main C&amp;C communicating function, etc.</p><h4><strong>String obfuscation</strong></h4><p>We came across samples of the core module with only cleartext strings but also samples with certain strings obfuscated by XORing them with a unique (per sample) hard-coded key.&nbsp;</p><p>Even within the samples that contain obfuscated strings, there are many cleartext strings present and there seems to be no logic in deciding which string will be obfuscated and which won’t. For example, most format strings are obfuscated, but important IoCs such as credentials or filenames are not.&nbsp;</p><p>To illustrate this: most strings in the function that retrieves a value from the comment.dat file are obfuscated and the call to <code>GetPrivateProfileStringW </code>is dynamically resolved by the <code>GetProcAddress </code>API, but all the strings in the function that writes into the same config file are in cleartext and there is a direct call to <code>WritePrivateProfileStringW</code>.&nbsp;</p><p>Overall, the core module code is quite robust and contains many failsafes and options for different scenarios (for example, the amount of possible protocols used for C&amp;C communication), however, we probably only saw samples of this malware that are still in active development as there are many functions that are not yet implemented and only serve as placeholders.</p><h3><strong>Plugins</strong></h3><p>In the section below, we will describe the functionality of the plugins used by the <code>Core Module (Proto8)</code> to extend its functionality.&nbsp;</p><p>We are going to describe three plugins with various functionalities, such as:</p><ul><li>Achieving persistence</li><li>Bypassing UAC</li><li>Registering an RPC interface</li><li>Creating a new account</li><li>Backdoor capabilities</li></ul><h4><strong>Core Plugin</strong></h4><p>0985D65FA981ABD57A4929D8ECD866FC72CE8C286BA9EB252CA180E280BD8755</p><p>This plugin is a DLL binary loaded by the fileless core module (<code>Proto8</code>) as mentioned above. It extends the malware’s functionality by adding methods for managing additional plugins. These additional plugins&nbsp; export the function <code>"GetPlugin"</code> which the core plugin executes.</p><p>This part uses the same command ID based calling convention as the core module (see above), adding three new methods:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h4><strong>Zload(Atomx.dll,xps1.dll)</strong></h4><p>2ABC43865E49F8835844D30372697FDA55992E5A6A13808CFEED1C37BA8F7876</p><p>The DLL we call <code>Zload </code>is an example of a plugin loaded by the <code>Core Plugin</code>. It exports four functions: <code>“GetPlugin”</code>, <code>“Install”</code>, <code>“core_zload”</code> and <code>”zload”</code>. The main functionality of this plugin is setting up persistence, creating a backdoor user account, and concealing itself on the infected system. We will focus on the exported functions <code>zload</code>, <code>core_zload</code> and the default <code>DllMain </code>function, as they contain the most interesting functionality.</p><h5><strong>Zload (process starter)</strong></h5><p>This function is fairly simple, its main objective is to execute another binary. It first retrieves the path to the directory where the <code>Zload </code>plugin binary is located <code>(&lt;root_folder&gt;)</code> and creates a new subfolder called<code> "mec" </code>in it. After this it renames and moves three files into it:</p><ul><li>the <code>Zload </code>plugin binary itself as<code> &lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\logexts.dll</code>,</li><li><code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\spdlogd.exe</code> as <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\spdagent.exe</code> and</li><li><code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\kb.ini</code> as<code> &lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\kb.ini</code></li></ul><p>After the files are renamed and moved, it creates a new process by executing the binary <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\spdagent.exe</code> (originally <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\spdlogd.exe</code>).</p><h5><strong>core_zload (persistence setup)</strong></h5><p>This function is responsible for persistence which it achieves by registering itself into the list of security support providers (SSPs). Windows SSP DLLs are loaded into the <code>Local Security Authority (LSA)</code> process when the system boots. The code of this function is notably similar to the <code>mimikat_ssp/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC</code> <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp/blob/master/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC.cpp">source code found on github</a>.</p><h5><strong>DllMain (sideloading, setup)</strong></h5><p>The default DllMain function leverages several persistence and evasion techniques. It also allows the attacker to create a backdoor account on the infected system and lower the overall system security.</p><h5><strong>Persistence</strong></h5><p>The plugin first checks if its DLL was loaded either by the processes <code>“lsass.exe”</code> or<code> “spdagent.exe”</code>. If the DLL was loaded by <code>“spdagent.exe”</code>, it will adjust the token privileges of the current process.</p><p>If it was loaded by <code>“lsass.exe”</code>, it will retrieve the path <code>“kb&lt;num&gt;.dll”</code> from the configuration file <code>“kb.ini”</code> and write it under the registry key <code>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\SYSTEM\\CurrentControlSet\\Services\\WinSock2\\Parameters</code> <code>AutodialDLL</code>. This ensures persistence, as it causes the DLL <code>“kb&lt;num&gt;.dll”</code> to be loaded each time the Winsock 2 library (<code>ws2_32.dll</code>) is invoked.</p><h5><strong>Evasion</strong></h5><p>To avoid detection, the plugin first checks the list of running processes for <code>“avp.exe”</code> (Kaspersky Antivirus) or <code>“NortonSecurity.exe”</code> and exits if either of them is found. If these processes are not found on the system, it goes on to conceal itself by changing its own process name to <code>“explorer.exe”</code>.</p><p>The plugin also has the capability to bypass the UAC mechanisms and to elevate its process privileges through <code>CMSTP COM</code> interfaces, such as <code>CMSTPLUA {3E5FC7F9-9A51-4367-9063-A120244FBEC7}</code>.</p><h5><strong>Backdoor user account creation</strong></h5><p>Next, the plugin carries out registry manipulation (details can be found in the appendix), that lowers the system’s protection by:</p><ul><li>Allowing local accounts to have full admin rights when they are authenticating via network logon</li><li>Enabling RDP connections to the machine without the user password</li><li>Disabling admin approval on an administrator account, which means that all applications run with full administrative privileges</li><li>Enabling anonymous SID to be part of the everyone group in Windows</li><li>Allowing <code>“Null Session”</code> users to list users and groups in the domain</li><li>Allowing <code>“Null Session”</code> users to access shared folders</li><li>Setting the name of the pipe that will be accessible to “Null Session” users</li></ul><p>After this step, the plugin changes the <code>WebClient </code>service startup type to <code>“Automatic”</code>. It creates a new user with the name <code>“DefaultAccount”</code> and the password <code>“Admin@1999!”</code> which is then added to the <code>“Administrator”</code> and <code>“Remote Desktop Users”</code> groups. It also hides the new account on the logon screen.</p><p>As the last step, the plugin checks the list of running processes for process names <code>“360tray.exe”</code> and <code>“360sd.exe”</code> and executes the file <code>"spdlogd.exe"</code> if neither of them is found.</p><h4><strong>MecGame(kb%num%.dll)</strong></h4><p>4C73A62A9F19EEBB4FEFF4FDB88E4682EF852E37FFF957C9E1CFF27C5E5D47AD</p><p>MecGame is another example of a plugin that can be loaded by the <code>Core Plugin</code>. Its main purpose is similar to the previously described <code>Zload</code> plugin – it executes the binary <code>“spdlogd.exe”</code> and achieves persistence by registering an RPC interface with <code>UUID {1052E375-2CE2-458E-AA80-F3B7D6EA23AF}</code>. This RPC interface represents a function that decodes and executes a base64 encoded shellcode.</p><p>The <code>MecGame </code>plugin has several methods for executing spdlogd.exe depending on the level of available privileges. It also creates a lockfile with the name <code>MSSYS.lck</code> or <code>&lt;UserName&gt;-XPS.lck</code> depending on the name of the process that loaded it, and deletes the files <code>atomxd.dll </code>and<code> logexts.dll</code>.</p><p>It can be installed as a service with the service name <code>“inteloem”</code> or can be loaded by any executable that connects to the internet via the <code>Winsock2</code> library.</p><h4><strong>MulCom</strong></h4><p>ABA89668C6E9681671A95B3D7A08AAE2A067DEED2D835BA6F6FD18556C88A5F2</p><p>This DLL is a backdoor module which exports four functions: <code>“OperateRoutineW”</code>, <code>“StartRoutineW”</code>, <code>“StopRoutineW”</code> and <code>”WorkRoutineW”</code>; the main malicious function being <code>“StartRoutineW”</code>.</p><p>For proper execution, the backdoor needs configuration data accessed through a shared object with the file mapping name either <code>“Global\\4ED8FD41-2D1B-4CC3-B874-02F0C60FF9CB”</code> or <code>"Local\\4ED8FD41-2D1B-4CC3-B874-02F0C60FF9CB”</code>. Unfortunately we didn’t come across the configuration data, so we are missing some information such as the C&amp;C server domains this module uses.</p><p>There are 15 commands supported by this backdoor (although some of them are not implemented) referred to by the following numerical identifiers:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h5><strong>Communication protocol</strong></h5><p>The <code>MulCom </code>backdoor is capable of communicating via HTTP and TCP protocols. The data it exchanges with the C&amp;C servers is encrypted and compressed by the RC4 and aPack algorithms respectively, using the RC4 key loaded from the configuration data object.</p><p>It is also capable of proxy server authentication using schemes such as Basic, NTLM, Negotiate or to authenticate via either the SOCKS4 and SOCKS5 protocols.</p><p>After successful authentication with a proxy server, the backdoor sends data xorred by the constant <code>0xBC</code>. This data is a set with the following structure:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/getacp.png.webp?itok=YfEG-j6N 1x, 1x, 2x" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Data structure</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Another interesting capability of this backdoor is the usage of layered C&amp;C servers. If this option is enabled in the configuration object (it is not the default option), the first request goes to the first layer C&amp;C server, which returns the IP address of the second layer. Any subsequent communication goes to the second layer directly.</p><p>As previously stated, we found several code similarities between the <code>MulCom </code>DLL and the <code>FFRat </code>(a.k.a. <code>FormerFirstRAT</code>).</p><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We have described a robust and modular toolset used most likely by a Chinese speaking APT group targeting gambling-related companies in South East Asia. As we mentioned in this blogpost, there are notable code similarities between <code>FFRat </code>samples and the <code>MulCom </code>backdoor. <code>FFRat </code>or <code>"FormerFirstRAT''</code> has been publicly associated with the<code> DragonOK group</code> according to the Palo Alto Network report, which has in turn been associated with backdoors like <code>PoisonIvy</code> and <code>PlugX </code>– tools commonly used by Chinese speaking attackers.</p><p>We also described two different infection vectors, one of which weaponized a vulnerable WPS Office updater. We rate the threat this infection vector represents as very high, as WPS Office claims to have <a href="https://www.wps.com/about-us/">1.2 billion installations worldwide</a>, and this vulnerability potentially allows a simple way to execute arbitrary code on any of these devices. We have contacted WPS Office about the vulnerability we discovered and it has since been fixed.</p><p>Our research points to some unanswered questions, such as reliable attribution and the attackers’ motivation.</p><h2><strong>Appendix</strong></h2><h3><strong>List of processes:</strong></h3><ul><li><code>360sd.exe</code></li><li><code>360rp.exe</code></li><li><code>360Tray.exe</code></li><li><code>360Safe.exe</code></li><li><code>360rps.exe</code></li><li><code>ZhuDongFangYu.exe</code></li><li><code>kxetray.exe</code></li><li><code>kxescore.exe</code></li><li><code>KSafeTray.exe</code></li><li><code>KSafe.exe</code></li><li><code>audiodg.exe</code></li><li><code>iexplore.exe</code></li><li><code>MicrosoftEdge.exe</code></li><li><code>MicrosoftEdgeCP.exe</code></li><li><code>chrome.exe</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Registry class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>Core module working directory (WD)</strong></h3><p>Default hard-coded WD names (created either in<code> C:\ProgramData\</code> or in <code>%TEMP%</code>):</p><ul><li><code>spptools</code></li><li><code>NewGame</code></li><li><code>TspSoft</code></li><li><code>InstallAtomx</code></li></ul><p>File used to test permissions: <code>game_&lt;tick_count&gt;.log</code> – the WD path is written into it and then the file is deleted.</p><p>Hard-coded security descriptor used for WD access: <code>“<em>D:(A;;GA;;;WD)(A;OICIIO;GA;;;WD)</em>”</code>.&nbsp;</p><p>Lockfile name format: <code>“&lt;working_dir&gt;\&lt;victim_username&gt;-&lt;comment_string&gt;.log”</code></p><p><strong>Core module mutexes:</strong></p><p><code>Global\sysmon-windows-%x</code> (%x is a CRC32 of an MD5 hash of the victim’s username)</p><p><code>Global\IntelGameSpeed-%x</code> (%x is a CRC32 of an MD5 hash of the victim’s username</p><p><code>Global\TencentSecuriryAgent-P01-%s </code>(%s is the victim’s username)</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li>&nbsp;Repository: <a Based on this, we suspect that the FFRat codebase is being shared between several Chinese adversary groups. Unfortunately, this is not sufficient for attribution as FFRat itself was never reliably attributed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this blogpost we will describe the malware used in these attacks and the backdoor planted by the APT group, as well as other malicious files used to gain persistence and access to the infected machines. the diagram above, we describe the relations between the malicious files. Some of the relations might not be accurate, e.g. we are not entirely sure if the MulCom backdoor is loaded by the \u003ccode\u003eCorePlugin\u003c/code\u003e. However, we strongly believe that it is one of the malicious files used in this campaign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Vector\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve seen multiple infection vectors used in this campaign. Among others, an attacker sent an email with an infected installer to the support team of one of the targeted companies asking to check for a bug in their software. In this post, we are going to describe another vector we’ve seen: a fake \u003ccode\u003eWPS Office\u003c/code\u003e update package. We suspect an attacker exploited a bug in the WPS updater\u003ccode\u003e wpsupdate.exe\u003c/code\u003e, which is a part of the WPS Office installation package. We have contacted WPS Office team about the vulnerability (\u003ca href=\"https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCVE-2022-24934\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/a\u003e), which we discovered, and it has since been fixed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring our investigation we saw suspicious behavior in the WPS updater process. When analyzing the binary we discovered a potential security issue that allows an attacker to use the updater to communicate with a server controlled by the attacker to perform actions on the victim’s system, including downloading and running arbitrary executables. To exploit the vulnerability, a registry key under \u003ccode\u003eHKEY_CURRENT_USER \u003c/code\u003eneeds to be modified, and by doing this an attacker gains persistence on the system and control over the update process. Windows SSP DLLs are loaded into the \u003ccode\u003eLocal Security Authority (LSA)\u003c/code\u003e process when the system boots. The code of this function is notably similar to the \u003ccode\u003emimikat_ssp/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC\u003c/code\u003e \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp/blob/master/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC.cpp\"\u003esource code found on github\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch5\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDllMain (sideloading, setup)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe default DllMain function leverages several persistence and evasion techniques. It also allows the attacker to create a backdoor account on the infected system and lower the overall system security.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch5\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePersistence\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe plugin first checks if its DLL was loaded either by the processes \u003ccode\u003e“lsass.exe”\u003c/code\u003e or\u003ccode\u003e “spdagent.exe”\u003c/code\u003e. If the DLL was loaded by \u003ccode\u003e“spdagent.exe”\u003c/code\u003e, it will adjust the token privileges of the current process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf it was loaded by \u003ccode\u003e“lsass.exe”\u003c/code\u003e, it will retrieve the path \u003ccode\u003e“kb\u0026lt;num\u0026gt;.dll”\u003c/code\u003e from the configuration file \u003ccode\u003e“kb.ini”\u003c/code\u003e and write it under the registry key \u003ccode\u003eHKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\\\SYSTEM\\\\CurrentControlSet\\\\Services\\\\WinSock2\\\\Parameters\u003c/code\u003e \u003ccode\u003eAutodialDLL\u003c/code\u003e. This ensures persistence, as it causes the DLL \u003ccode\u003e“kb\u0026lt;num\u0026gt;.dll”\u003c/code\u003e to be loaded each time the Winsock 2 library (\u003ccode\u003ews2_32.dll\u003c/code\u003e) is invoked.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch5\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEvasion\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo avoid detection, the plugin first checks the list of running processes for \u003ccode\u003e“avp.exe”\u003c/code\u003e (Kaspersky Antivirus) or \u003ccode\u003e“NortonSecurity.exe”\u003c/code\u003e and exits if either of them is found. It also hides the new account on the logon screen.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs the last step, the plugin checks the list of running processes for process names \u003ccode\u003e“360tray.exe”\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003e“360sd.exe”\u003c/code\u003e and executes the file \u003ccode\u003e\"spdlogd.exe\"\u003c/code\u003e if neither of them is found.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMecGame(kb%num%.dll)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003e4C73A62A9F19EEBB4FEFF4FDB88E4682EF852E37FFF957C9E1CFF27C5E5D47AD\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMecGame is another example of a plugin that can be loaded by the \u003ccode\u003eCore Plugin\u003c/code\u003e. Malware Authors Study Algebra: The Group Theory Inside Bedep's DGA","created":"1776878106","subtitle":"A closer look at how Bedep used foreign exchange data and advanced math to generate 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTLDR: \u003c/strong\u003eBedep was a malware family active mainly in 2014 and 2015 that used an unusually sophisticated domain generation algorithm, or DGA, to hide its command-and-control infrastructure. Instead of relying only on the date, it used real foreign exchange rates published by the \u003cem\u003eEuropean Central Bank\u003c/em\u003e, making its future domains much harder to predict in advance. Under the hood, the malware used ideas from group theory to generate a fixed set of unique domains without collisions, a rare example of advanced mathematics being applied directly in real-world malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost domain generation algorithms are boring. A linear congruential generator, a date seed, some modular arithmetic, a character table -- \u003ccode\u003eseed = (seed * 0x41C64E6D + 0x3039) % 2^31\u003c/code\u003e, rinse, repeat. You reverse them in an afternoon, pre-compute next month's domains over lunch, and move on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep author implemented a number theory textbook inside a 32-bit DLL, and the result is one of the most mathematically elegant pieces of malware ever shipped.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis post is a deep dive into this unique engineering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat is Bedep?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep is an ad-fraud botnet that was active from late 2014 through 2015, delivered exclusively through the Angler exploit kit. When a Flash zero-day (CVE-2015-0311) was burning, Angler was dropping Bedep. The malware itself was a modular downloader focused on click fraud, but its infrastructure is what made it interesting. In early 2015, new variants appeared with a DGA for C2 resolution. A three-day sinkhole by Arbor Networks (ASERT) caught phone-homes from roughly 82,000 unique IPs, spread across every continent except -- notably -- Russia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDennis Schwarz originally reversed the DGA at ASERT, who published a proof-of-concept Python reimplementation and a detailed write-up titled \u003cem\u003e\"Bedep's DGA: Trading Foreign Exchange for Malware Domains.\"\u003c/em\u003e The original blog post has since been taken down from NETSCOUT's site, but survives on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The PoC lived at \u003ccode\u003egithub.com/arbor/bedep_dga\u003c/code\u003e. Dennis got the algorithm working but noted that parts of it were opaque -- he called the core transform a \"blackbox\" and flagged the embedded table as \"likely something Fermat number related.\" He was right. Let's open that box.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ECB trick: Seeding a DGA from the financial markets\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore the math starts, Bedep needs a seed that both the bot and the botmaster can independently derive without communicating. Most DGAs use the date. Bedep uses the date \u003cem\u003eand\u003c/em\u003e the global foreign exchange market.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm fetches two XML files from legitimate public services:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec5890edf5187711796f804fae7ad8577\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUTC time\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003eearthtools.org/timezone/0/0\u003c/code\u003e -- the current timestamp, converted to \"days since year zero.\"\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead6313dc967de96442575ff50339b412\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEuro foreign exchange reference rates\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003ewww.ecb.europa.eu/stats/eurofxref/eurofxref-hist-90d.xml\u003c/code\u003e -- the European Central Bank's daily publication of EUR conversion rates against 31+ world currencies, updated every business day.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe time is used to select which day's rates to use. Specifically, Bedep picks \"last Tuesday's\" exchange rates -- meaning the preceding week's Tuesday until Thursday, after which it rolls forward to the current week's Tuesday. Only rates falling on a Monday boundary (in the algorithm's day-counting scheme) are considered. This gives a roughly weekly rotation with a deterministic selection rule.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom the chosen date, Bedep extracts up to 48 currency rates -- USD, JPY, GBP, CZK, BGN, HUF, and so on. Each rate is parsed through a slightly broken custom \u003ccode\u003eatof()\u003c/code\u003e implementation (off by a bit in the least significant digits -- a quirk, not a feature), packed into a 64-bit IEEE double, and its low 32-bit dword is extracted for use in the algorithm.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere is what a single run looks like, from the PoC output for April 7, 2015:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparsed 31 currencies from 2015-04-07 (currency date): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;USD: 1.0847 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;JPY: 130.33 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; BGN: 1.9558 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CZK: 27.455 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DKK: 7.4714 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GBP: 0.7286 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HUF: 299.08 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PLN: 4.0578 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;RON: 4.4165 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SEK: 9.374 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; CHF: 1.0438 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NOK: 8.73 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HRK: 7.619 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; RUB: 59.8265 \u0026nbsp; TRY: 2.8079 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;AUD: 1.4192 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;BRL: 3.3979 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CAD: 1.3563 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CNY: 6.7241 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HKD: 8.4086 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;IDR: 14091.77 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ILS: 4.267 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; INR: 67.598 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;KRW: 1183.28 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;MXN: 16.1919 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; MYR: 3.9527 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NZD: 1.4423 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PHP: 48.3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SGD: 1.4724 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;THB: 35.335 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ZAR: 12.8345\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhy is this clever? Because the ECB rates are:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5f3c952abb440c715bcfa626ac9bd4c0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePublicly available\u003c/strong\u003e -- anyone can fetch the same XML\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7730a0d72c71be00a0c3d3feebc10618\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eGlobally consistent\u003c/strong\u003e -- every bot on every continent gets the same data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1667d4c4edd48ffbda05118e24153bf0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnpredictable in advance\u003c/strong\u003e -- nobody can predict next Tuesday's EUR/USD rate\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e80b9125dab13aae37cb9ed8b88656475\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHistorically verifiable\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB publishes archives going back years\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d9bf0abba3c6cb7703705ce6203f707\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eImpossible to tamper with\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB is not going to modify their reference rates to help you sinkhole a botnet\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd because the malware fetches this from \u003ccode\u003eecb.europa.eu\u003c/code\u003e, the request looks like legitimate traffic. A Snort signature (SID 33188) was written to detect it, but it fired constantly on real users checking exchange rates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe mathematical core\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is where Bedep stops being a clever botnet and starts being a discrete mathematics exercise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePrecomputed subgroup parameters\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL embeds a lookup table -- 102 entries of 8 dwords each, stored as JSON in the PoC (\u003ccode\u003etransform2_table_varN.json\u003c/code\u003e, one per config variant). At first glance it looks like random constants. It is not.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform2\u003c/code\u003e function takes a hash derived from the exchange rates and days-since value, then uses it as an index into this table. Each table entry, after being decoded through config-specific XOR and multiply constants (\u003ccode\u003e0x663d81 * entry[0] ^ value3\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003e0x281 * entry[1] ^ value2\u003c/code\u003e), yields two critical values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee4084ac1a9ee922a9a2585119d02acb9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ep\u003c/strong\u003e -- a prime number\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7d298adce011a8fe31f7d5db7f1685ee\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eq\u003c/strong\u003e -- an integer such that q divides p - 1\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the subgroup structure. The table stores primes whose group orders (p - 1) have known factorizations with small prime factors. The malware doesn't need to factor p - 1 at runtime -- that hard work was done offline by the author and baked into the DLL. Dennis Schwarz noted these were \"likely something Fermat number related,\" and he was onto something: the primes are chosen so that their totient has a smooth factorization, making generator-finding tractable.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe number of domains to generate comes directly from q - 1. For the first config, this works out to 22 domains; for the second, 28 -- totaling 50 domains per weekly rotation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFinding generators: A textbook primitive root search\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith p and the prime factorization of p - 1 in hand, Bedep needs a generator of the multiplicative group ((\\mathbb{Z}/p\\mathbb{Z})^*) -- or more precisely, of a specific subgroup of order q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform8\u003c/code\u003e function does this in two stages. First, it runs a \u003cstrong\u003etrial-division prime sieve\u003c/strong\u003e: starting from 3, stepping by 2 (skipping evens), it tests each candidate against all previously found primes. It collects up to 37 small primes, then filters for those that actually divide q (the subgroup order). This gives the prime factorization of q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e finds the \u003cstrong\u003eprimitive root\u003c/strong\u003e itself. This is the standard algorithm straight from a number theory textbook:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003edef find_primitive_root(p, prime_factors_of_order): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for g in range(2, p // 2): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = True \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for q_i in prime_factors_of_order: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# If g^((p-1)/q_i) ≡ 1 (mod p), then g generates \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# a proper subgroup, not the full group. Reject it. \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if pow(g, (p - 1) // q_i, p) == 1: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = False \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;break \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if is_generator: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return g \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return None\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe actual code in \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e is this logic rendered in C-style loop structure with manual index tracking, but the mathematics is identical. For each candidate g starting from 2, it checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e[ g^{(p-1)/q_i} \\not\\equiv 1 \\pmod{p} \\quad \\text{for all prime factors } q_i \\text{ of } p-1 ]\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf g passes all checks, it generates the full group. This is literally the algorithm from Section 11.1 of Shoup's \u003cem\u003eA Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra\u003c/em\u003e, implemented in a malware DLL.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWalking the cyclic group\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow comes the payoff. With a prime p, a generator g, and a subgroup order q, the \u003ccode\u003etransform7\u003c/code\u003e function sets up the DGA's iteration state:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emodulus \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;= p \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_c seed \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g, random_e, p) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_4 -- starting element step \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g2, random_f, q) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # field_8 -- step exponent\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe starting position \u003ccode\u003eseed\u003c/code\u003e is g raised to a random exponent (derived from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e, the CPU timestamp counter). The step is similarly randomized from a second generator. Both are computed via modular exponentiation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen, for each domain, the iteration is simply:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed = pow(seed, step, modulus) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # one step through the cyclic group domain = seed_to_domain(seed, currencies)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThat single line -- \u003ccode\u003epow(seed, step, modulus)\u003c/code\u003e -- is the entire DGA engine. Each call advances the state to the next element of the cyclic subgroup.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause the subgroup has order q, and step is coprime to q (guaranteed by the generator construction), this walk visits every non-identity element of the subgroup exactly once before cycling. The number of domains generated equals q - 1, which is precisely the number of non-identity elements. No collisions. No wasted iterations. No off-by-one hoping you hit enough domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe key insight:\u003c/strong\u003e the set of group elements visited is deterministic (fixed by p, g, and the exchange rates), but the \u003cem\u003eorder\u003c/em\u003e of visitation depends on the random exponents from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e. Every infected machine walks through the same set of q - 1 elements, generating the same q - 1 domain names, but potentially in a different order. Same destinations, different paths.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eflowchart TB \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph cyclic_group [\"Cyclic Subgroup of (Z/pZ)*\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E1[\"g^1 mod p\"] --\u0026gt; E2[\"g^2 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E2 --\u0026gt; E3[\"g^3 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E3 --\u0026gt; E4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E4 --\u0026gt; Eq[\"g^(q-1) mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Eq --\u0026gt; E1 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph walk [\"DGA Walk (order depends on rdtsc)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1[\"seed_0\"] --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S2[\"seed_1\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S3[\"seed_2\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S4 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| SN[\"seed_(q-2)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1 -.-\u0026gt;|\"= g^e mod p\\n(random start)\"| cyclic_group \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| E3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| Eq\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eFrom Group Elements to Domain Names\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach group element is a 32-bit integer. Turning it into a domain name is the final step, handled by \u003ccode\u003etransform11\u003c/code\u003e. This is less mathematically elegant and more of a traditional mixing function, but it has a nice property: every character position in the domain uses a \u003cem\u003edifferent\u003c/em\u003e currency rate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm cycles through the full list of parsed currencies (31 in the example above). For a domain of length 18, that means character 0 is influenced by USD, character 1 by HRK, character 2 by MXN, character 3 by GBP, and so on. Each character is computed by:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1b4d60fdb8fc1f153baf8a99855a0b35\"\u003eMultiplying the currency's 3-letter name (packed as a 32-bit int) and its 64-bit floating-point rate by large constants\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec05b47f97cd63ed0295b790fd5474c85\"\u003eXORing and shifting the result with the current seed and the days-since value\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2f1e2c5d769b5e8978ca12f1d05cf791\"\u003eTaking the result modulo 26 (for positions 2+) or modulo 36 (for the last two positions, allowing digits)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e589b38ae5aa632fba755388fc26bd71c\"\u003eAdding the ASCII offset for 'a'\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe domain length itself is derived from the seed XORed with a rate-dependent value, bounded between 12 and 18 characters. All domains use the \u003ccode\u003e.com\u003c/code\u003e TLD.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA trace from the PoC output shows the mixing in action:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed: 0xa13c9652 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in USD's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in HRK's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in MXN's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in GBP's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;... \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in ZAR's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;domain: rrpohktjlscncqxvt3.com seed: 0x558af439 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in USD's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;... \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;domain: wjavcjhazzxyxotkbi.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eEach domain is a fingerprint of the entire exchange rate vector, filtered through one specific element of the cyclic group.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Full Pipeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003ePutting it all together:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eflowchart LR \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ECB[\"ECB XML\\n31+ currency rates\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect[\"Select last\\nTuesday's rates\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Earth[\"earthtools.org\\nUTC timestamp\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DateSelect --\u0026gt; Mix[\"XOR/multiply\\nrates + days_since\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Mix --\u0026gt; TableLookup[\"Lookup table\\n→ prime p, order q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;TableLookup --\u0026gt; GenFind[\"Sieve primes,\\nfind generator g\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GenFind --\u0026gt; GroupSetup[\"seed = g^rand mod p\\nstep = g2^rand mod q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GroupSetup --\u0026gt; Walk[\"Iterate:\\nseed = seed^step mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Walk --\u0026gt; CharMix[\"Mix seed with\\ncurrency rates\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CharMix --\u0026gt; Domains[\"22-28 domains\\nper config\"]\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eSeven configs were observed in the wild, each with its own embedded table, XOR constants, and currency count (36 or 48). Each malware variant embedded two configs that ran per rotation -- the first generating 22 domains, the second 28, yielding 50 domains total. Across the observed campaign, domains were registered 2-5 days ahead of use through \"Domain Context\" registrar with Regway nameservers, all resolving to infrastructure at 5.196.181.244 and 46.105.251.1.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCompare Bedep's approach with its contemporaries:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eFamily\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDGA Technique\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eSeed\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eConficker.C\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + simple arithmetic\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCurrent date\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGamaredon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNested character-range loops\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHardcoded ranges\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNecurs\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCRC32 hash of date components\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + constant\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMatsnu\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDictionary word concatenation\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDays since epoch\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBedep\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCyclic group walk mod p\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eECB exchange rates\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery other DGA on this list can be pre-computed arbitrarily far into the future. Bedep cannot -- because Tuesday's exchange rates don't exist until Tuesday. You can sinkhole Conficker's domains for next year during your coffee break. For Bedep, you have to wait for the ECB to publish, then race the botmaster to register the domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe mathematical guarantee is real: the cyclic group structure ensures exactly q - 1 distinct domains per rotation with no collisions and no wasted iterations. The primitive root construction ensures the generator produces every element. The precomputed tables avoid expensive factorization at runtime. The exchange rate seed provides unpredictable entropy from a trusted, globally-consistent, publicly-verifiable source.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not someone who copy-pasted a DGA from a forum. The author understood multiplicative groups modulo primes, knew how to find primitive roots, precomputed smooth-order primes offline, and wired the whole thing to the European Central Bank's daily publications. Whether they learned this from a university course, a textbook, or a cryptography library's source code, the result is unmistakable: this is applied algebra, deployed in production, at scale.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe original ASERT analysis is preserved on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The proof-of-concept implementation was published on \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/arbor/bedep_dga\"\u003eASERT's GitHub\u003c/a\u003e. The sample analyzed here is \u003ccode\u003ee5e72baff4fab6ea6a1fcac467dc4351\u003c/code\u003e (MD5) /\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ed0fb1b66b6e4da395892327be9f39adb4533e7759ace39f67bdde0bb1cdaef35\u003c/code\u003e (SHA256).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eCredits: The DGA was originally reversed by Dennis Schwarz at Arbor Networks / ASERT.
