420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIn the spring of 2022, our anti-rootkit engine flagged something unusual inside a svchost.exe process on a machine in the United Kingdom. A chunk of memory -roughly 32 kilobytes- was making direct NtQuerySystemInformation syscalls, bypassing every usermode hook we had in place. When we pulled the memory dump and opened it in a disassembler, we found not just shellcode, but an entire virtual machine: a custom bytecode interpreter running a 25-kilobyte program, decoding its own payload on the fly, resolving APIs through hash lookups, and checking for hypervisors before executing a single line of its real code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe named it AngrySpark -after the C++ namespace angry_spark found in the RTTI metadata of the DLL that delivered it. Over the following year, we captured three memory snapshots from the same host, watched its C2 infrastructure evolve, and eventually saw the connection get blocked. Then it vanished. No new samples. No new infrastructure. No second victim. Just a single spark in the dark.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the story of how we took it apart.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy AngrySpark matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost backdoors pick a lane. AngrySpark built three of them. The DLL, the VM, and the beacon each carry their own encryption, their own API resolution, and their own C2 channel. Compromise one layer and the other two remain opaque.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat architecture is what elevates it from a well-built backdoor to something worth writing up. The payload is assembled from bytecode at runtime, so the operator can swap out the final-stage binary, change the API set, add anti-analysis checks, or rotate C2 URLs by pushing a new 25KB blob. The 6.5KB loader never changes. It behaves more like a deployment framework than a conventional dropper.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe other reason it matters is the mismatch between effort and scope. The engineering is serious: a VM-based deployment framework, dual encrypted C2 channels, CET-aware anti-analysis, runtime code patching, and direct syscalls with a 24-build compatibility table. Yet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNo public malware repository carried the DLL during its operational life, and no other vendor published on it. That does not make AngrySpark more important than broader campaigns, but it does make it unusually revealing. It looks like high-effort malware deployed with a very limited visible footprint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe architecture at a glance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark operates as a three-stage system. A DLL masquerading as a Windows component loads via the Task Scheduler, decrypts its configuration from the registry, and injects position-independent shellcode into svchost.exe. That shellcode implements a virtual machine. The VM processes a 25KB blob of bytecode instructions, decoding and assembling the real payload -- a beacon that profiles the machine, phones home over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can receive encrypted shellcode for execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach stage is designed to be independently replaceable. The VM's bytecode format means the operator can swap out the payload, change the configuration, rotate C2 URLs, or add new anti-analysis checks -- all without modifying a single byte of the loader code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStage\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRole\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eKey idea\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 0, DLL loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMasquerades as a Windows Task Scheduler extension, decrypts config from the registry, and injects shellcode into svchost.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA quiet entry point with persistence, configuration handling, and self-removal logic\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 1, VM loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRuns a custom bytecode interpreter that assembles the real payload at runtime\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eThe operator can change behavior by updating the blob rather than the loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 2, beacon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eProfiles the host, communicates over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can execute encrypted shellcode from the server\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA full C2 channel built to blend in and stay adaptable\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 0, the DLL loader\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter loading its configuration, the DLL injects the VM shellcode into a svchost.exe service host process. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 1, the virtual machine\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries (VM instructions) encoded in a custom format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (syscall tables). Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 2, the beacon\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eStealth, anti-analysis, and self-removal\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark is not only modular, it is also careful about how it appears to defenders. Several design choices look specifically aimed at frustrating clustering, bypassing instrumentation, and limiting the forensic residue left behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe binary's PE metadata has been deliberately altered to confuse toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eNotable defensive friction points\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed531a883985e6ff1ea0b5526cc40abfc\"\u003eThe PE timestamp is falsified, and the Rich header was modified after compilation in a way that breaks straightforward toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9519384a4b6896277de1f1a911e912fd\"\u003eThe shellcode uses direct syscalls to get around usermode API hooks.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eee6db62c8e4b4429a19949b6ac9524f9\"\u003eThe VM includes hypervisor checks and CET or Shadow Stack aware anti-analysis logic.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0bba18ae1c14f5eb447d8061bc1654bc\"\u003eThe DLL pins the C2 server's TLS certificate fingerprint, rejecting connections to anything that does not match the expected infrastructure. If someone sinkholed the domain or stood up a proxy with a different certificate, the DLL would refuse to talk.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecbf5d179746f6984a71674a8cdd15326\"\u003eThe loader can remove itself if contact goes stale or if the server explicitly tells it to do so.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The operator built the backdoor to vanish automatically if it lost its handler -- which is exactly what happened in April 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eCommand and control design, and how the operation evolved\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 channels don't share anything except a hosting provider. AES-256-CBC + RSA-4096 on one side, XXTEA + custom Base64 + PNG steganography on the other. Different encryption, different protocols, different domains, different IPs, all within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range. The custom Base64 alphabet rotates with each configuration update, so there's no fixed encoding to write a signature against.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePoint in time\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWhat changed\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eApril to June 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe timeline suggests active maintenance in mid-2022, followed by a freeze in the shellcode and continued rotation only in the configuration and network paths. Then the infrastructure stopped being renewed. That sequence matters because it makes the disappearance look operational, not accidental.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat we still do not know\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eYet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired. The DLL never surfaced in any public malware archive. No other vendor has published on it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWas it a targeted operation that achieved its objective and was burned? A test deployment that was abandoned? A contractor's proof of concept? We don't know.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we do know is that somewhere in the RTTI metadata of a tampered DLL, someone chose the namespace angry_spark and the exception class sombrely_exception. And populated it with nine C++ classes: BCryptSymmetricKey, BCryptAsymmetricKey, BCryptKey, crypto_exception, connection_exception, wininet_exception, angry_spark_exception, bad_config_exception, and -- curiously -- sombrely_exception. They built a virtual machine to deploy their payload, wrapped it in three layers of encryption, hid their commands inside PNG images, and then -- quietly, sombrely -- let it all expire.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe spark went out. But the code remains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor readers who want the deeper reverse engineering detail, the appendix below preserves the underlying technical observations, tables, and selected code fragments from the working draft in a more reference-oriented format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Appendix\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix A. Detailed Stage 0 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL was never uploaded to VirusTotal. It remained invisible to the public threat intelligence ecosystem for the entirety of its operational life.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL stores its encrypted configuration in the Windows registry under a path that itself is encrypted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA heartbeat timestamp (FILETIME) at bytes 0-7 of the registry value is updated after each successful beacon, allowing the operator to track the last check-in time.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The operator built the backdoor to vanish automatically if it lost its handler -- which is exactly what happened in April 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter loading its configuration, the DLL injects the VM shellcode into a svchost.exe service host process. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eClaimed tool Detailed Stage 1 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries encoded in a custom format:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM implements 22 distinct opcodes -- a complete deployment toolkit:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA typical blob is processed in phases:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery string, API name, DLL name, and code chunk in the blob is encoded with a cascading XOR cipher. The format is deceptively simple:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e[total_length: 1 byte][data: N bytes][checksum1: 1 byte][checksum2: 1 byte]\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two checksum bytes double as XOR keys. Decoding works backwards through the data, using separate key chains for even and odd byte positions. After decoding, the original checksums are recomputed using seeds 0x23 and 0xA5 and verified against the saved keys. If either mismatches, the entry is rejected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe maximum decoded payload is 253 bytes (limited by the single-byte length field). This is why the 27KB payload requires 93 separate DECODE_COPY instructions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM resolves all APIs by walking the Process Environment Block (PEB), iterating loaded modules, and comparing DJB2 hashes of export names:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = 5381\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003efor byte in name:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = (hash * 33 + byte) \u0026amp; 0xFFFFFFFF\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo variants exist: one for UNICODE_STRING (with case folding via | 0x20), used to find DLLs by name, and one for ASCII byte strings, used to match export table entries.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCategory\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOpcodes\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWhat INIT_STATE\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eVirtualAlloc a 53KB buffer, set region permissions, zero-fill sections\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eData buffer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRelocations\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRELOCATION\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eFix up 10 QWORD pointers by adding the allocated base address\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAPI resolution\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLOAD_DLL, RESOLVE_API\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eXOR-decode DLL/API names, resolve via GetModuleHandle + GetProcAddress\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eExecution\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCALL_VOID, CALL_RETURN\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eInvoke functions in the assembled payload\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eConfiguration\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCONFIG_KV\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWrite 16 key-value pairs (C2 URLs, crypto keys, base64 alphabet)\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eIntegrity\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eINTEGRITY (x15 entries)\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDJB2 hash checks on critical bypass usermode API hooks, the loader includes a syscall instruction wrapper with a hardcoded build-number-to-syscall-number table:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe extracted and compared the syscall 7 / SP1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9200\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9600\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8.1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e10240-19044\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 1507-21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e20344\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 Insider (Fe/Co)\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e--\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e22000\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin11 21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (table at offset 0x1312). Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM scans msvcrt.dll's code sections for a specific 12-byte x64 instruction pattern:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8D 15 XX XX XX XX lea rdx, [rip + offset] ; string ptr\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8B C8 mov rcx, rax\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eFF 15 YY YY YY YY call [rip + offset] ; indirect call\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a match is found, the VM XOR-decodes a target string, compares it against the data at the lea target, and if it matches, captures the indirect call target -- provided it points into KERNELBASE.dll or KERNEL32.dll. This hijacks specific CRT internal function pointers for exception dispatch, allowing the loader to intercept control flow without modifying the exception handler chain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore any payload code runs, the VM applies several checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe RDSSPQ instruction reads the Shadow Stack Pointer. Intel's Control-flow Enforcement Technology (CET) is primarily found in instrumented environments and certain hypervisors, so a non-zero SSP is a strong signal that the code is running under analysis or in a hardened VM.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring our emulation of the VM bytecode, three instruction types initially defied classification:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eType 21 is the anti-sandbox kill switch. The 4-byte parameter controls it: when set to 0, the VM checks for a hypervisor (via CPUID) and a non-zero Shadow Stack Pointer (via the RDSSPQ instruction). If both are detected on Windows 10 version 2004 or later, the entire loader aborts with error code 0xAACFBB1A. This is a targeted anti-sandbox technique -- most analysis environments running recent Windows versions with hardware-assisted security features would trigger this check.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBlob Type\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRaw Byte\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eResolution\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 0\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x01\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAllocate Memory -- VirtualAlloc the 53KB execution buffer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 17\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x12\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eException Handler Data -- stores pointer to XOR-encoded API name block used during exception setup\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x16\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAnti-Analysis -- conditional abort if hypervisor present + CET Shadow Stack active + OS ≥ Win10 2004\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix C. Detailed Stage 2, infrastructure, and configuration notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNine data collectors run in sequence, each appending results to a session packet:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe process enumeration has a fallback chain. It first tries the Toolhelp API (CreateToolhelp32Snapshot, Process32FirstW/Process32NextW). If that fails, it falls back to NtQuerySystemInformation with information class 5 (SystemProcessInformation), walking the linked list of SYSTEM_PROCESS_INFORMATION structures. Each process name is hashed with MD5 (via the CryptoAPI), and the hash is added to the session packet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCollected data flows through a multi-stage encryption pipeline before transmission:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe XXTEA implementation uses the standard 32-round Feistel structure with a 128-bit key derived from the configuration. The custom Base64 alphabet changes with each configuration update, which defeats signature-based network detection without adding any complexity to the protocol.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eGET /assets/static/img/I4o8Pp_41.png HTTP/1.1\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eHost: pick.storewebzone.net\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eUser-Agent: wininet/10.0.19044\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCache-Control: no-cache\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eConnection: Keep-Alive\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe server's response contains actual PNG data, but with a custom chunk type. The payload scans for the magic byte 0xA1 followed by a 24-byte header:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe payload uses a packet protocol with 22 entry types for serializing collected data:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach entry is prefixed with a type byte and length, forming a TLV (Type-Length-Value) structure that the C2 server parses to reconstruct the victim's profile.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe parent domain server-sys.com was registered in June 2021 through Tucows with WHOIS privacy protection. Several related subdomains (ss1., api., bs.) were observed on the same IP, suggesting purpose-built infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth IPs fall within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range, suggesting the operator rented multiple VPS instances from the same UK-based hosting provider.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM writes 16 configuration key-value pairs into the payload's data segment:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo distinct XXTEA keys serve different purposes: key #1 encrypts outbound data (system profiles), while key #2 decrypts inbound payloads (shellcode from the server). This separation means compromising one key doesn't expose the other direction of communication.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThree memory snapshots, each captured months apart, tell the story of a living operation:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable software is often designed to hide its true behavior, wrapping its underlying logic in layers that make it difficult to analyze. “Unpacking” is the process researchers use to peel back those layers and reveal what the software is actually doing. But when standard tools run into unfamiliar or custom protections, this process can quickly become slow and highly manual. This project explores whether AI can help bridge that gap, assisting with the more repetitive parts of analysis so researchers can focus on validation, reasoning, and the parts of reverse engineering that still require human judgment.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAutomated static binary unpacking is hard, and it tends to stop being hard in a useful way only when the packer is familiar. There are good unpackers out there, including some internal ones, but once you get out of their comfort zone, unsupported packers, custom routines, or slightly unusual logic can bring the whole process to a halt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat makes unpacking a good example of where\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/engineering-the-future-of-agentic-threat-hunting\"\u003eAI may genuinely help\u003c/a\u003e. Across our broader work at Gen, we are increasingly using\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/engineering-the-future-of-agentic-threat-hunting\"\u003eAI-assisted workflows\u003c/a\u003e to accelerate parts of reverse engineering, technical correlation, and evidence organization, not as a substitute for expert analysis, but as a way to reduce friction in the slower, more repetitive parts of the job. The responsibility for verification and final conclusions still stays with the researcher.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom that perspective, the idea behind what we call the Last-Resort Unpacker is fairly simple: if AI is good at spotting patterns and translating logic across representations, could it help us recover payloads in cases where traditional static unpacking pipelines fail? We are not trying to solve the hardest forms of protection here. The goal is more pragmatic, to cover more real-world cases, reduce manual effort, and potentially extract logic that can later feed back into our regular unpacking workflows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe came up with two approaches.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHigh level design – Variant 1\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven a sample to unpack, this simple premise has a few steps. Since we’ll go the most direct but also the riskiest way, let us call it \u003cem\u003ea carpe diem\u003c/em\u003e variant.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIdentification:\u003c/strong\u003eThe first step is to identify which functions might be responsible for the unpacking and which buffers they are targeting. We have decided to use Gemini 2.5 Pro for our workload and complemented it with \u003cem\u003eradare2\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003ereverse engineering framework to provide structured access to the disassembly. Once we identify the suspected decryption routine and the corresponding buffers, we may proceed to the translation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTranslation:\u003c/strong\u003e The next step relies on Gemini’s capabilities to rewrite the identified functions into their pythonic representation with prescribed function signatures.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVerification:\u003c/strong\u003e Now we have a Python code that hopefully faithfully represents the machine code identified in the first step. Now comes up the time to admit that we will be cheating on the initial assignment and we have to ensure that we will not be caught. We can easily statically check whether the source code is a valid source code. Now comes the tricky part – we have to verify that the product Python code does not have undesirable side-effects, be it from simple hallucinations or worse – due to hypothetical prompt injection. We have decided to do a strict abstract syntax tree (AST) verification which permits only certain keywords, operations, imports, and calls. If we obtain something that does not pass these criteria, then we have an interesting research case. In the other case, we may proceed to unpacking.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnpacking:\u003c/strong\u003e Using the verified script, we attempt to do extraction on the source sample. Being paranoid, we shall use read-only environment with limited network access. If we succeed, we have not only an unpacked buffer but also an extraction script that we can use to improve our regular unpacking capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHigh level design – Variant 2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow that we have attempted to do the most straightforward way, we can try to be a bit cleverer. Given the assumption that we can identify parts of the code responsible for the unpacking, adding two more assumptions that the unpacking code is localized and does not utilize any sophisticated system calls we could try to utilize emulator which should be a safe alternative that does not suffer from hallucinations. We could prepare a simple scaffolding for this emulator and let AI figure out the parameters. This would isolate non-deterministic behavior to the first step only.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIdentification:\u003c/strong\u003eThe first step is almost identical but instead of identifying functions responsible for unpacking, we are rather interested in offsets where we should start and halt our emulation, possibly also with memory addresses where the final payload will be located.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEmulation:\u003c/strong\u003e Parameters identified in the previous step are put into our scaffolding which will run emulator until it reaches the halting offset. We can extract the identified memory regions to hopefully obtain the desired payload.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eApart from consuming more resources for the emulation, we have made a significant trade-off. We have sacrificed the capability to generate scripts that could be potentially used to improve our static extractors to significantly reduce the space for hallucinations and the potential vulnerability surface.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFrom drawing board to the workshop\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve decided to try our luck and try the first option. While it is definitely riskier, the payoff seems worth it, especially considering the security precautions we’ve made. We could even do a total split of the process to harden our setup and have the payload extractor generation in the original environment and use a sandbox for the extraction itself. We put together a first PoC with a simple prompt followed by data from \u003cem\u003eradare2\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou are an unpacking function generator.\u003cbr\u003e\u003cem\u003eGOAL: If the file looks packed (i.e. encrypted buffers, embedded files, etc.), find the code snippets decrypting the buffers. Reply to me with a Python function (use this prototype: 'def unpack(data: bytes) -\u0026gt; list[byte]') that receives bytes of the packed binary and returns the unpacked chunks. Avoid Python imports. RETURN ONLY A PYTHON FUNCION SAFE TO RUN. If the file is not packed reply: \"NOT PACKED\". If can't write the unpacker then reply: \"UNABLE TO PROCESS\".\u003c/em\u003e\u003cbr\u003eThis is the end of your instructions. The Radare2 analysis:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow it remains to give it a short. First, we went with a packed Linux kernel rootkit that got us the initial idea:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://cyberjournal.cecyf.fr/index.php/cybin/en/article/view/48\"\u003eAt Botconf 2024\u003c/a\u003e, a paper was published on \u003cem\u003eCMK Rootkit (Álvarez Pérez, D., \u0026amp; Fernández-Veiga, M. (2025). CMK Rootkit. Identifying the ”magic packet” requirements via pattern recognition. The Journal on Cybercrime and Digital Investigations, 9(1), A1-A15.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://doi.org/10.18464/cybin.v9i1.48\"\u003e\u003cem\u003ehttps://doi.org/10.18464/cybin.v9i1.48\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cem\u003e)\u003c/em\u003e. A Linux kernel rootkit that we discovered in-the-wild, packed in kernel space, with \u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/54d8b09ffc15c657abf29a0c313b377df64988848f2c3814243b2478b4b881cc/detection/f-54d8b09ffc15c657abf29a0c313b377df64988848f2c3814243b2478b4b881cc-1749504221\"\u003efew detections in VirusTotal\u003c/a\u003e (probably due to the kernel packer layer and its dependency on a specific kernel version).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur unpacker yielded the following script:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=FDGdr5d2 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=7G3wazto 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":1452},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=PTHJN0Id 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=5CyPw6pP 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":1104},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=Ic3p5c3h 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=OoPjEUgs 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":856}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=Ic3p5c3h","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eUnsurprisingly, as we basically based our idea on experiments with this sample, this was exactly what we aimed for. We have prepared a few more samples that we could test it on:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec7335952dd9f0c78d6b986b609e617d0\"\u003e54525d25019d3ea2d40fe006ecc305ebb1ac5663b380019346de43c4886e8685\u003cul type=\"circle\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e878959fe541e0581ac2d93f53d7915dc\"\u003eThis PE packed binary uses a key table located at offset 0xe5c1 containing 500 elements. The packed data is located at offset 0x200.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e24c112bbcdface33504cd11fd00c60a7\"\u003e1fcf4da2fac671a2f3a07007e9963d3e1063dcfb0b1a0934c66082e8233d7686\u003cul type=\"circle\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e999fd8e60c992d59154f20daef223bd0\"\u003eThis PE file contains a payload encoded as a word-based substitution cipher. It implements anti-sandbox protection that checks the available memory (via GlobalMemoryStatusEx) and the system ticks (via GetTickCount64) to determine if the underlying system is a sandbox.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eClosing thoughts\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis experiment fits into a broader shift in how we are using AI in technical research. The value is not in handing over reverse engineering to a model and hoping for the best. It is in identifying narrow, high-friction parts of the workflow where pattern recognition and code translation can save analysts time, while validation, judgment, and final conclusions remain firmly in human hands.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA last-resort unpacker will not solve the hardest cases, and it is not a replacement for established tooling or expert analysis. Advanced multi-layer protections, virtualization, and heavily environment-dependent logic still remain difficult territory. But even within those limits, the approach already shows practical value. If it helps recover payloads from unsupported packers, reduces manual effort in repetitive cases, or produces reusable logic that can later improve our existing unpacking pipelines, then it is worth pursuing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSeen from that perspective, the point is not that AI is replacing reverse engineers. It is that it can make experienced researchers faster when conventional automation runs out of road.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-04/Untitled%20design%20%2810%29.jpg?h=197483aa\u0026itok=FBTi1OvU","language":"en","heroImage":{"default":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_hero_extra_wide/public/2026-04/Untitled%20design%20%2810%29.jpg.webp?h=197483aa\u0026itok=gsVfCtF1 points\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4b810198ac9891110da1771c3f2d0397\"\u003eGen Threat Labs has identified Remus, a new 64-bit infostealer we attribute to the infamous Lumma Stealer family – emerging in the wake of Lumma’s takedown and the doxxing of its alleged core members.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ede8006689bbfaba0d6fa856478b3ea79\"\u003eIn this technical blog post, we detail the compelling evidence tying Remus to Lumma across multiple dimensions.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef3f0d531ccf019ab00b98a38b3c43c17\"\u003eWe also describe a previously undocumented Application-Bound Encryption bypass employed specifically by Remus and Lumma.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee0040d0e9463ac082d24fcdbfd7af133\"\u003eThe first Remus campaigns date back to February 2026, with the malware switching from Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers to EtherHiding and employing new anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the security industry talks about information stealers, Lumma Stealer, without a doubt, has become the notorious icon of this landscape. Not only could it count itself among the most sophisticated, technically advanced, and widespread stealers-as-a-service in the world, but it was also described in a variety of blog posts from basically everyone in the industry, including us.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this analysis, we describe a new variant of Lumma Stealer that we call Remus, a new x64 build which we suspected might occur in the foreseeable future after the doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025. And the future is here.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus brings the same stealing arsenal to the table as we already know from Lumma, capable of stealing stored browser passwords, cookies, cryptocurrency, and much more. However, rather than revisiting Lumma's well-documented capabilities, we focus on the remarkable resemblance between Remus and Lumma, as well as the new techniques Remus introduces, including the use of EtherHiding to resolve C2s, replacing the traditional use of Steam and Telegram dead drop resolvers, and additional anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attribution of Remus to Lumma is many-fold and described thoroughly throughout the blog post. The main indicators, to name a few, are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used explicitly by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith that said, we can still see active Lumma campaigns all around the world, which makes Remus not a replacement, but a continuous evolution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDevelopment timeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that during our hunting efforts, we came across several test samples, which even carry a \u003ccode\u003etestbuild\u003c/code\u003e label embedded directly in the binaries (see Figure 1). We are internally referencing these builds as Tenzor (based on encrypted strings). The build timing closely coincides with a period when Lumma Stealer suffered a major blow through a doxxing campaign that exposed alleged core members and severely disrupted its operations. This may suggest that some of Lumma’s authors split off, or that Lumma decided to rebrand under a new name in the midst of the doxxing fallout. Nevertheless, at Gen Threat Labs, we track both Remus and Tenzor as variants of Lumma Stealer. That said, the only samples bearing the Tenzor name are the ones that also carry the \u003ccode\u003etestbuild\u003c/code\u003e label. All others are identified as Remus.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=QbujBO5R 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=Iam4JdKV 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":261},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=qDlIpY-R 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=jyL-DQiI 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":199},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=9pwE8FGW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=aVJ4659W 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":154}],"caption":"Figure 3: Development timeline.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_03_0.jpg.webp?itok=9pwE8FGW","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eSimilarities between Remus and Lumma\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen we first started analyzing Remus, something felt familiar, and for good reason. Many of the techniques and design patterns we encountered in Remus closely mirrored those we had already seen in Lumma Stealer. The main difference is that Lumma was 32-bit while Remus is 64-bit, which naturally introduced some variations. Yet, when we looked past the 32-bit versus 64-bit differences and stripped away the obfuscation layers, what remained was strikingly familiar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the following subsections, we highlight the most compelling evidence of a direct connection between Remus and Lumma. Note that this is not an exhaustive list. The overlaps are far more numerous, but we selected just a few that we believe speak for themselves.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eForgotten dead drop resolver and string obfuscation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a malware researcher, strings are among the most valuable resources during reverse engineering, and in the case of attributing Remus to Lumma, they proved no different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma use a virtually identical mechanism for string obfuscation. The encrypted string is first assembled on the stack using a series of various forms of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction, followed by a decryption loop that transforms the data byte by byte. The transformation is often further obscured by MBA (Mixed Boolean-Arithmetic) obfuscation and is either inlined or placed in a standalone function. In any case, each string is protected using a unique transformation, making it very difficult to build a universal static decryptor. As a result, emulating the decryption loops is likely the most effective approach for dealing with encrypted strings in both Remus and Lumma.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=gXkouPhN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=VYItjdSJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":760},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=RSIG-gtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Sx7JfLS1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":578},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Kc1NFRAj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=BYluRXHH 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":448}],"caption":"Figure 4: Decryption of the string “Processes.txt” in Remus (left) and Lumma (right). However, unlike most techniques relying on injection, Lumma as well as Remus do not use this shellcode to call \u003ccode\u003eIElevator::Decrypt\u003c/code\u003e with the key obtained from the \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e file (the \u003ccode\u003e[“os_crypt”][“app_bound_encrypted_key”]\u003c/code\u003e JSON field). Instead, it locates the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly in the browser’s process memory, where it is stored in an encrypted form protected by \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. It then calls the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the same flag from within the browser’s context to decrypt it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is important to note that this in-memory encrypted form of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is protected with a \u003cstrong\u003edifferent key\u003c/strong\u003e than the one used to protect the key on disk – it is a separate protection layer that Chromium browsers use to safeguard the key while it resides in memory at runtime. However, since the key is protected with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, decryption can only be performed by the same process that encrypted it, which is precisely why injection into the browser process is necessary in this specific bypass. For the curious, we discussed this topic in more detail in our recent\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVoidStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom this point on, we will walk through the ABE bypass as implemented in Remus, noting any differences from Lumma’s implementation where applicable. Where no differences are explicitly mentioned, the two implementations work either identically or very similarly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo how does Remus find the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e? It begins by walking the browser’s PEB module list, looking for two modules in a single pass: \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e and the browser DLL (e.g., \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e is found, Remus manually parses its PE export directory, reading the DOS header, PE header, and export table through repeated \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e syscalls. For each exported function name, it computes a seeded CRC32 hash and compares it against the pre-computed hash\u0026nbsp;corresponding to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Essentially, a typical API-hashing, but in a remote process. Once a match is found, it\u0026nbsp;resolves the function’s RVA to obtain its absolute address in the browser’s address space.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the browser DLL is found during the same pass, Remus decrypts a hex pattern \u003ccode\u003e488d058bcc8d02488901488b024889415b488d41\u003c/code\u003e, which was encrypted by the same string obfuscation technique described earlier and stores the value \u003ccode\u003e0xEFF87\u003c/code\u003e (which corresponds to \u003ccode\u003e11101111111110000111\u003c/code\u003e in binary) into a variable we labeled as \u003ccode\u003ewildcard_mask\u003c/code\u003e (see\u0026nbsp;Figure\u0026nbsp;8).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_08_0.jpg.webp?itok=RG69h1KY While their implementations differ in some details, mostly as a natural consequence of the 32-bit vs 64-bit architecture, the overall design is strikingly similar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the first things both do upon execution is that they enumerate all \u003ccode\u003eNt\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed exports from \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e and build a lookup table mapping their name hashes to the corresponding Syscall Service Numbers (SSNs). The process is the same in both cases: they walk the \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e export directory, hash each matching export name, and scan the first 32\u0026nbsp;bytes of each function’s prologue to extract the SSN from the \u003ccode\u003emov eax, \u0026lt;SSN\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e instruction. Both Remus and Lumma begin executing their malicious logic directly from the PE entry point (the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003estart\u003c/code\u003e\u0026nbsp;function), with no runtime initialization whatsoever. In a typical C/C++ binary, the entry point calls the CRT startup routine, which sets up the heap, initializes global variables, registers exception handlers, processes the command line, and invokes static constructors before eventually calling\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emain\u003c/code\u003e. However, neither Remus nor Lumma follows this pattern. Instead, both jump directly from the entry point into their core logic: resolving modules and APIs, performing anti-analysis checks, initializing C2 communication, and exfiltrating data, ending with a direct call to \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e. This suggests both were compiled without linking to the standard C runtime, likely a deliberate choice to minimize the binary size and reduce unnecessary dependencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe structural similarities extend further. Both binaries share the same section layout, with each section serving an identical semantic role. Even the C2 configuration follows the same pattern: in both cases, the encrypted blob, key, and nonce are statically embedded in the \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and decrypted at runtime using ChaCha20.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo summarize, we strongly believe the degree of similarity between the two codebases is too deliberate to be a coincidence, pointing to a single, shared origin.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eIndirect control flow obfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFinally, both Remus and Lumma make use of control flow obfuscation by replacing direct jumps with indirect ones, where the target is read from an offset stored in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section. As shown in Figure 28, this technique takes several forms. In its simplest case, the target address is resolved by a single pointer dereference (highlighted in yellow). In more complex cases, it extends to a full jump table, where a computed index selects among several target addresses stored consecutively in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section (highlighted in orange). Lastly, in some cases, the target address is first loaded into a register (highlighted in blue) and then jumped to via a register-based indirect jump (highlighted in pink).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=yw52wiVm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=IzpQtf-7 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":526},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=p9HiY3wT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=SsanKuYo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":400},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=nkn_NNA0 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":310}],"caption":"Figure 28: Randomly selected disassembly code from Remus (left) and Lumma (right) highlighting the different forms of the discussed indirect control flow obfuscation.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAlthough functionally equivalent to normal jumps, the indirection\u0026nbsp;complicates the control flow analysis in two ways. First,\u0026nbsp;some of the indirect jump targets cannot be statically determined (for example, when the register holding the target address can be set in multiple different preceding basic blocks, each potentially loading a different target), leaving the control flow graph incomplete as the connections between basic blocks are lost. Second, some of these orphaned blocks end up being misidentified as standalone functions, further disrupting function boundary detection and leading to a broken, incomplete decompiler output. Although removing the obfuscation entirely requires more effort, the IDA’s decompiler output can be significantly improved by marking the referenced offsets as constants. For those interested, we covered this trick in our \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/defeating-aurastealer-obfuscation\"\u003eAuraStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e, along with other approaches for tackling control flow obfuscation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat’s new in Remus\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving covered the key similarities between Remus and Lumma, let’s now focus on what is new in Remus. The core architecture is clearly inherited from Lumma. Still, several changes go beyond a straightforward 32-bit-to-64-bit port. These range from refactored strings and more thorough device fingerprinting to a different exfiltration archive format, a switch from FNV-1a to CRC32 for API hashing, and an overhauled C2 communication that has been restructured to deliver dynamic configurations incrementally rather than in a single large blob. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most significant changes, however, are the replacement of traditional Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers with blockchain-based C2 resolution using EtherHiding, a persistence technique allowing attackers to store malware-related data (such as domains, payloads, encryption keys, among others) on blockchain as a smart contract, and the introduction of additional anti-analysis checks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDead drop resolvers via EtherHiding\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma employ dead drop resolvers, a mechanism in which the malware does not contact its C2 server directly but instead retrieves the C2 address at runtime from an intermediary hosted on a legitimate platform. This makes the infrastructure significantly more resilient, as if a C2 domain is taken down, the dead drop can be adjusted to point to a new server without the need to distribute an updated binary. Moreover, the dead drop URLs typically reside on well-known, high-reputation services, making them more difficult for security vendors to block without collateral damage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere the two differ is in the choice of platform. Lumma relies on Steam profiles and Telegram channels, typically encoding C2 URLs using ROT-15, while Remus goes a step further, replacing these with Ethereum smart contracts. At runtime, it sends an \u003ccode\u003eeth_call\u003c/code\u003e JSON-RPC request to a hardcoded contract address via a public RPC endpoint and extracts the C2 URL from the hex-encoded response. Because blockchain data is decentralized and immutable, there is no platform operator to report abuse to, making the dead-drop effectively immune to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJust like Lumma’s Steam profile URL, Remus stores the smart contract address separately from the static C2 configuration, as a stack-encrypted string. Notably, after the JSON response is retrieved, the C2 address is, at least for now, merely hex-encoded in the \u003ccode\u003eresult\u003c/code\u003e field (no other obfuscation, such as ROT-15 in case of Lumma, is applied).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=aykiX20l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=pL76kfRZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":670},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=YSc7ZWVs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=Y3V3btE1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":509},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=FFY3enyR 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=mSV0E7oS 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":395}],"caption":"Figure 30: Remus resolving a C2 using EtherHiding. If either check fails, the malware silently terminates via \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first check targets sandbox and analysis tool DLLs. We focused on various similarities and differences between Lumma and Remus, thoroughly describing the technical aspects of both. need Photoshop for a side project. The subscription is expensive. You search for a free Mac download, land on a site that looks good enough, and within an hour, the app is running. A few weeks later, your Mac is hot even when you're not doing much. The fans stay loud. The battery drains fast. Activity Monitor shows a process with a name you do not recognize, chewing through CPU in the background. That \"free\" app may have cost you your machine's processing power, your saved passwords, your browser sessions or access to the files on your disk. That trade is more common than many Mac users think.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIn the first 48 hours after we began tracking one recent cracked software distribution wave, our protections blocked about 108,000 attempts to launch these applications on macOS systems.\u003c/strong\u003e This is a count of blocked launch attempts recorded by one of our shields, not unique devices. Of those detections, 58% came from Macs still supported by Apple, while 42% came from systems that no longer receive Apple's current support. The pattern is already clear: even for MacOS, cracked apps are still a practical delivery channel for cryptominers, infostealers, adware and long-term persistence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe signature that keeps showing up\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpen enough cracked Mac apps and you start to see recurring patterns. One of the patterns we see quite often is a string left by the TNT group:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e.TNT - why join the navy if you can be a pirate\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStill, this story will not be about them but rather the whole distribution pipeline around them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA crack may start as a modified version of a legitimate app with license checks removed. After that, it often moves through mirror sites, torrent uploads, forum reposts, Telegram channels and re-packed archives. Every handoff is another chance to add something extra to the file. That is the real problem. Users rarely download a package from a trusted source. They are downloading whatever the last person in the chain decided to upload.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy people still download cracked Mac apps\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCost is one of the obvious reasons. A subscription can feel absurd when you need an app for one class, one freelance project or one weekend task. There is another reason too: old hardware. Plenty of people still use older Macs that work well enough for everyday tasks, but no longer run the latest macOS release. Once that happens, the legitimate software market starts shrinking around them. New versions no longer support old releases; official downloads for old versions get harder to find and compatibility drops. The machine still works, but the software ecosystem has moved on.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly when cracked software starts to look practical. It promises a version that runs on old hardware, costs nothing and solves the problem immediately. What it does not say is that older Macs are often already at a disadvantage. They may miss newer security features and recent patches. If the installation also tells the user to weaken built-in protections, the system becomes much easier to abuse.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow cracked apps reach users\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCracked macOS software spreads through a loose supply chain. Some files come from dedicated download sites that look almost like real app stores. Others spread through torrent trackers, warez forums, Telegram channels, Discord communities and file-sharing links posted on SEO-driven \"download\" pages. The packaging changes, the mirrors change and the hostnames change. The model stays the same.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe important point is not which site was involved. It is that every re-upload, re-zip and re-pack creates another opportunity to insert a payload. The original crack may be clean, but the version on a mirror site may already tell a different story; the torrent may include an extra installer, the archive posted to a chat channel may have been modified again. There is usually no reliable way for a typical user to tell the difference before running it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=VBfED1MK 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=XxWeLl0- 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":908,"height":854},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=Vb6lPM6t 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=KcYWsV5C 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":837},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=_clBtlLq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=fi2aKjgx 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":649}],"caption":"Every step between the original crack and the final download is an opportunity to add malware or unwanted software. MacOS includes several protections that are meant to stop exactly this kind of software from running freely. Gatekeeper helps block apps from unidentified developers. Notarization checks software distributed outside the App Store for known malicious content. System Integrity Protection, or SIP, helps prevent changes to critical parts of the system. Permission controls limit what apps can read and modify. And cracked software install guides often tell users to work around those protections step by step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTypical instructions often include:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae2da4be03f3a2b670f4a813338399f9\"\u003eDisable Gatekeeper so unsigned apps can run.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb606b7c024052b2cb4a67f27b385f2ce\"\u003eBoot into Recovery Mode and disable SIP.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4a9d11e409c539f6414805c6023c5d8\"\u003eLaunch the crack tool with administrator privileges.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e10349530c6710071bdfe76740183e59c\"\u003eGrant Full Disk Access when prompted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/terminal-screenshot.jpg.webp?itok=28oaj3UT
